## Document 064: 2026年9月经济数据点评：总量企稳的读数，结构分化的底色

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026年9月经济数据点评：总量企稳的读数，结构分化的底色.pdf
Report source: 西南证券
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

请务必阅读正文后的重要声明部分  
1 
[Table_ReportInfo] 2026 年 10 月 19 日      
证 券研究报告 •宏观简评报告      
数据点评  
总量企稳的读数，结构分化的底色  
——2026 年 9 月经济数据点评

点评

西 南证券研究 院 
[Table_Summary]  摘要：GDP方面，2026年第三季度我国 GDP 同比增长 4.5%，较二季度回升
0.2 个百分点；前三季度增长 4.6%，仍处于全年 4.5%-5%的目标区间内。出
口和高技术制造业是主要支撑，全球 AI 产业景气带动电子产业链中游需求保
持强劲，但投资、消费恢复偏慢。预计四季度GDP 同比增长 4.5%-4.7%，出
口韧性与政策支持有望托底， 但地产调整和消费偏弱仍将制约经济增长。居民
收支和就业方面，前三季度居民人均可支配收入名义增长5.3%，增速较上半
年回升；人均消费支出名义增长 3.6%，消费增速未随收入同步加快。前三季
度城镇调查失业率平均为5.21%， 稳就业、促增收和职业技能培训政策有望改
善居民收入预期， 但消费倾向回升仍需观察。工业生产方面，前三季度规上工
业增加值同比增长 5.3%，9 月单月增长 5.5%，较 8 月提高 0.3 个百分点。高
技术产业和装备制造业增加值分别增长14.3%和 10.2%，AI 电子链、专用设
备及船舶航空等行业表现较强，但部分高景气产品增速放缓。预计全年规上工
业增加值增速为 5.2%-5.4%， 高技术制造业、 装备制造业及出口仍是主要支撑。
固定资产投资方面，前三季度固定资产投资同比下降 7.2%，扣除房地产开发
投资后下降 4.2%。高技术产业投资增长 4.9%。基建投资同比下降 4.2%，但
信息传输、 航空运输和水上运输业投资保持较快增长。剩余专项债、地方债结
存限额及新型政策性金融工具有望支撑基建和制造业投资。房地产投资方面，
前三季度房地产开发投资同比下降20.6%，9 月单月下降 27.19%，降幅继续
扩大。新开工、 竣工面积分别下降24.9%和 25.1%，商品房销售面积、销售额
分别下降 13.2%和 13.9%， 房企到位资金下降21.6%， 行业供需和资金面仍然
承压。9 月房价环比表现有所改善，购房贷款贴息政策也有望降低居民购房成
本。预计四季度销售降幅可能在贴息政策和低基数带动下收窄，但趋势性回暖
仍需观察。制造业投资方面， 前三季度制造业投资同比下降2.1%， 降幅较1-8
月收窄 0.2 个百分点；9 月单月下降 0.95%，降幅明显收窄，但部分改善可能
来自前期需求集中释放和低基数。铁路、船舶、航空航天及其他运输设备制造
业投资增长 15.8%，计算机、通信和其他电子设备制造业增长9.6%。设备工
器具购置增速由一季度的 13.9%回落至前三季度的 9.7%，显示“两新”政策
拉动有所减弱。 预计四季度制造业投资单月增速在零附近波动，累计降幅有望
收窄至 1.5%-2.0%，行业分化或将延续。消费方面，前三季度社零同比增长
1.1%， 商品和服务零售总额增长2.6%， 其中服务零售额增长5.0%， 快于商品
零售的 1.0%。9 月单月社零增长 1.1%，较 8 月回升 0.7 个百分点，中秋错期
和低基数对增速形成提振。通讯器材类和烟酒类零售额分别增长 31.1%和
13.8%，而家电类增长 1.9%、汽车类下降 21.2%，汽车消费仍是主要拖累。
去年同期集中购车推高基数，今年补贴力度收缩、 部分需求提前释放，进一步
制约汽车消费。预计全年社零增速中枢约 1.4%；四季度低基数有望带动增速
小幅回升，但消费整体仍处于低位企稳阶段，趋势性复苏动力不足。 
 总量落在目标区间，出口走强与内需拖累并存。按不变价计算，2026 年第三
季度我国 GDP同比增长 4.5%，较二季度回升 0.2 个百分点，高于 Wind 一致
预期的 4.41%。2026 年前三季度 GDP 同比增长 4.6%，较上半年回落 0.1 个
百分点， 但仍高于Wind一致预期的 4.56%， 增速仍处于全年4.5%-5%的预期
目标区间内。 分产业看， 第三季度第一、 二、 三产业同比分别增长3.6%、3.4%

和 5.2%，其中第一产业增速回落 0.1 个百分点，二、三产业增速均回升。从
增长动力看，出口和高技术制造业是三季度经济增长的重要支撑。 受益于全球
AI 产业景气， 电子产业链中游产品需求保持强劲， 前三季度进出口表现超出市
场预期， 高技术产品出口的竞争优势持续巩固。与出口和制造业生产的较强韧
性相比，内需表现仍相对偏弱，投资和消费恢复偏慢，经济增长的内生动力有
待增强。三季度名义GDP 增速约为 6%，GDP 平减指数连续两个季度为正，
三季度初步核算为 1.33%，价格因素对名义经济增长的支撑有所增强。但价格
回升仍主要集中在上游能源和AI 相关行业，PPI回升的同时，CPI仍受居民消
费需求偏弱影响，整体处于低位。这表明上游涨价向下游商品和服务价格的传
导仍不充分，居民收入与消费之间也尚未形成明显的正向循环，价格回暖向内
需扩散仍需时间。预计出口韧性与政策支持仍是四季度 GDP的主要支撑。全
球 AI产业链景气有望带动四季度出口维持较高增速； 同时，5500亿元地方债

结存限额、PSL 利率下调至 1.5%并支持“六张网”建设，以及 8000亿元新
型政策性金融工具持续投放， 有望推动基建项目形成更多实物工作量， 并对制
造业投资形成支撑。 但以旧换新政策的拉动效应有所减弱， 房地产市场仍处于
深度调整阶段，内需恢复仍面临约束。整体来看，四季度经济仍将呈现外需支
撑较强、内需修复偏慢的格局。
 居民消费修复存在滞后性，就业景气保持平稳。从收入来看，2026 年前三季
度全国居民人均可支配收入同比名义增长 5.3%，扣除价格因素后实际增长
4.4%，增速较上半年均有所回升， 居民收入增长有所改善。从消费支出看，前
三季度全国居民人均消费支出同比名义增长3.6%，增速较上半年回落 0.1 个
百分点，实际增长 2.7%，与上半年持平，收入增速回升尚未同步转化为消费
增长， 居民消费修复节奏有所滞后。从消费结构看，前三季度食品烟酒、 居住、
医疗保健消费支出同比增速分别回升至4.47%、1.89%和 2.39%，其余消费支
出细项增速均较上半年边际回落， 反映消费支出的边际改善主要集中于部分日

常生活及刚性支出，其他消费领域的增长动能仍显不足，居民消费修复的广泛
性有待增强。就业方面，前三季度全国城镇调查失业率平均值为5.21%，较上
半年回升 0.01 个百分点，9 月全国城镇调查失业率为5.2%，环比下降 0.1 个
百分点， 短期就业状况有所改善， 但前三季度整体就业形势尚未出现明显变化。
10 月 10 日，国新办发布会相关负责人介绍， “十五五”时期将加大投资于人
力度，力争在此期间开展补贴性职业技能培训5000 万人次，新增取得技能证
书 4000 万人次、其中取得高级工以上技能证书1400 万人次。稳就业、促增
收与职业技能培训政策持续落地，居民就业稳定性及收入预期有望边际改善，
但考虑到前三季度收入增速回升而消费支出增速未同步加快， 居民消费倾向的
改善仍有待观察， 全年消费增长或延续温和修复态势。就业方面，预计城镇调
查失业率总体保持平稳， 后续就业政策对重点群体就业和劳动者技能提升的支
持力度有望进一步加大，但就业结构性矛盾的缓解仍需一定时间。
 单月工业生产边际改善，AI中间品强劲而消费电子承压。2026年前三季度，

全国规模以上工业增加值同比增长5.3%，增速与 1-8 月持平。9 月单月，工业
增加值同比增长 5.5%，较8 月提高 0.3 个百分点，符合 wind 一致预期 （5.5%）。
工业生产动能有所修复， 但整体增速尚未明显加快。前三季度高技术产业工业
增加值累计同比增长14.3%，较 1-8 月提升 0.1 个百分点；装备制造业工业增
加值累计同比增长 10.2%，均明显高于规上工业整体增速， 高技术相关产业仍
是工业增长的主要支撑。分三大门类看，9 月单月，制造业增加值同比增长
6.0%，较 8 月回落 0.1 个百分点； 电力、热力、燃气及水生产和供应业同比增
长 5.6%，采矿业同比由 8 月的下降转为上升 1.3%，增速均边际回升。三大门
类生产表现有所分化， 工业生产的边际改善尚未体现为各领域同步加速。从细
分行业看，9 月单月，计算机、 通信和其他电子设备制造业、专用设备制造业，
以及铁路、 船舶、 航空航天和其他运输设备制造业同比分别增长16.4%、12.1%、
11.7%，在主要行业中表现靠前。这反映 AI 电子链、 设备更新相关产业及船舶

航空等领域生产较为活跃，工业增长对高技术中间品、资本品及部分出口链条
的依赖度有所上升。石油和天然气开采业同比回升 3.6 个百分点至 9.8%，回
升幅度居前。通用设备制造业、电气机械及器材制造业、汽车制造业等制造业

9 月增速保持在 6%以上，但增速相比 8 月边际回落。分产品看，9 月单月，

但内需修复偏缓或制约增长动能进一步扩散。
 整体基建投资持续承压，新动能与新基建是结构性亮点。2026 年前三季度，

全国固定资产投资（不含农户）同比下降 7.2%，与 1-8 月增速持平。扣除房
地产开发的固定资产投资下降 4.2%，地产端仍是拖累整体投资的重要因素。
其中，知识产权产品投资同比增长9.3%。高技术产业投资同比增长4.9%，其
中信息服务业，航空、航天器及设备制造业， 电子及通信设备制造业投资分别
增长 20.2%、12.5%、7.6%，说明新质生产力、数字基础设施、航空水运等方
向仍有扩张。根据历史数据倒推测算，9 月单月， 固定资产投资同比下降9.69%，
降幅较 8 月单月收窄 3.82 个百分点，本轮边际修复或主要来自基建与制造业
端的积极变化。基建投资方面，统计局数据显示，前三季度基础设施投资同比
下降 4.2%，降幅相比 1-8 月扩大 0.2 个百分点。其中，信息传输业、航空运
输业、水上运输业投资分别增长 30.8%、13.8%、13.5%。根据历史数据测算，
前三季度广义基建投资同比下降 6.08%，降幅扩大 0.71 个百分点。9 月单月，
广义基建投资同比下降 10.48%，降幅收窄 4.3 个百分点。基建投资单月边际

改善可能来自低基数、天气扰动消退、专项债集中发行后项目资金到位。从资
金端看，截至 10 月 16 日，2026年超长期特别国债 1.3万亿元已经发行完毕，
与去年进度基本持平。9 月单月，地方政府新增专项债发行6204 亿元，同比
增速扩大至 50.18%，截至 9 月末发行进度 80.62%，略慢于去年同期。截至
10 月 16 日，今年地方政府专项债已发行 3.65 万亿元，年内剩余发行额度为
7546 亿元。在低基数、 天气扰动消退，以及剩余专项债、5500亿元地方债务
结存限额和 8000 亿元新型政策性金融工具加快落地形成实物工作量的支撑
下，年内投资单月降幅有望继续收窄。但资金到实物工作量有时滞、冬季施工
约束仍在，全年投资增速或将大幅收窄降幅、靠近零增长区间， 出现明显正增
的概率不大。
 地产市场仍处调整阶段，投资和销售继续走弱。2026 年前三季度，全国房地
产开发投资累计同比下降20.6%，降幅较 1-8 月扩大 0.7个百分点。根据历史
数据倒推测算，9 月单月，房地产开发投资同比下降27.19%，降幅相比 8 月

扩大 1.05 个百分点，说明房企开发活动仍然偏弱，房地产投资对整体投资的
拖累继续存在。供给端，相比于 1-8 月，房屋施工面积同比下降 12.8%，降幅
与上月持平；新开工面积和竣工面积同比降幅分别扩大至 24.9%和 25.1%；需
求端， 相比于 1-8 月，商品房销售面积和销售额降幅分别 扩大至 13.2%和
13.9%。资金端，前三季度房企到位资金同比下降 21.6%，降幅扩大 0.6个百
分点。其中国内贷款下降 33.1%，是各项来源中萎缩最快的一项，外部融资持
续承压、销售回款同步萎缩，从而制约房企资本开支。价格端，9 月单月，70
个大中城市中，一线城市商品住宅销售价格环比继续上涨， 二三线城市环比降
幅总体收窄，一二三线城市同比降幅均收窄，环比上涨城市个数比上月增加，
价格端的结构性改善与总量收缩并存。1-9 月二手房网签面积 6.17亿平方米，
同比增长 10.7%，已超过同期新建商品房销售面积5.65 亿平方米，需求载体
向二手房切换的特征进一步显现。政策方面，财政自 10 月 1 日起对符合条

件的新发放首套住房商业性个人住房贷款按 本金给予年化 1 个百分点、最
长 5 年的贴息。这是财政首次将贴息工具直接用于居民购房贷款，标志着
政策着力点正式由企业和投资端向居民终端需求延伸。 展望四季度， 购房贴
息叠加低基数效应， 有望推动商品房销售同比降幅收窄， 但销售趋势性回暖仍

需观察。 投资端受房企销售回款偏弱、 现房销售改革可能拉长资金回笼周期等

 制造业投资降幅收窄，结构性修复特征突出。2026 年前三季度，制造业投资
累计同比下降 2.1%，降幅较 1-8 月收窄 0.2 个百分点，增速在连续五个月边
际下降后边际回升。根据历史数据倒推测算，9 月单月，制造业投资同比下降
0.95%，降幅相比上月收窄5.11个百分点。9 月投资降幅收窄或受前期需求集
中释放和去年同期基数较低的影响。分行业看，13 个主要行业中，铁路、船
舶、航空航天和其他运输设备制造业投资增速最高，前三季度投资同比增速为
15.8%，计算机、通信和其他电子设备制造业（+9.6%）、纺织业（+4.2%）增
速居前， 电气机械及器材制造业（+1.2%）、 通用设备制造业（+1.1%）同比亦
实现正增长，且增速相比1-8 月边际回升。但需要注意，中游装备制造业投资

的改善， 主要受到出口需求和设备更新政策的带动。 设备工器具购置增速已从
一季度的 13.9%回落至前三季度的 9.7%，说明“两新”政策的拉动作用正在
减弱。金属制品业（-10.1%） 、食品制造业（-10.3%）仍处于深度收缩区间，
是制造业投资的主要拖累因素，主要由于地产及消费链投资内生动能偏弱。预
计四季度制造业投资单月增速将在零附近波动，累计降幅有望进一步收窄至
1.5%-2.0%，但全年累计增速转正的难度仍较大。分行业看，航空航天、电子
设备等行业 有望继续支撑制造业投资，制造业投资的分化格局可能延续。
 高基数与中秋错期抬升读数，消费仍处于低位磨底阶段。2026 年前三季度，
全国社会消费品零售总额同比增长 1.1%，与 1-8 月增速持平，除汽车以外的
消费品零售额同比涨幅扩大 0.1 个百分点至 2.8%，说明汽车消费仍为消费增
速的拖累点。根据统计局披露， 前三季度社会消费商品和服务零售总额同比增
长 2.6%，其中服务零售额增长5.0%，增速相比 1-8 月加快 0.1 个百分点，商

品零售额增长 1.0%，增速持平 1-8 月表现。服务零售中，通讯信息服务类、
旅游咨询租赁服务类、文体休闲服务类零售额较快增长。9 月单月，社会消费
品零售总额同比涨幅扩大 0.7 个百分点至 1.1%，主要受中秋错期叠加低基数
影响。其中，9 月餐饮收入同比涨幅扩大 1 个百分点至 2.1%，商品零售同比
扩大 0.7 个百分点至 1%。从具体类别看，9 月单月主要类别商品零售同比增
速均边际回升。其中，通讯器材类、烟酒类同比分别增长 31.1%、13.8%，相
比 8 月增速分别回升 3.8、1.3 个百分点。通讯器材高增，主要由于手机新品在
9 月集中发布拉动换机需求释放，同时全年 2500 亿元以旧换新资金在 9 月全
部下达，换机需求提前兑现。家电和汽车是仅有的两类增速回落品类：家电类
零售额同比增长1.9%， 增速较8 月回落 0.4个百分点； 汽车类同比下降21.2%，
降幅扩大 2.7个百分点。汽车类消费收缩，主要由于去年四季度前多地以旧换
新补贴额度接近用尽，消费者集中购车，推动汽车单月销量创近年同期新高。

今年补贴力度有所收缩， 叠加去年集中购车对部分需求形成提前释放，汽车消
费增长因此承压。此外，石油及制品类、体育娱乐用品类、家具类、建筑及装
潢材料类、金银珠宝类等前期累计增速回落的品类，9 月单月增速边际回升幅
度均超 2 个百分点。该回暖主要依托中秋错期、低基数效应、价格因素与季节
性窗口。整体来看， 年内消费有望保持低位企稳， 趋势性回升的动力仍显不足。
四季度低基数效应有望带动社零增速小幅回升， 但汽车消费面临去年同期高基
数、以旧换新补贴力度减弱等压力，低基数带来的提振作用或较为有限。
 风险提示：政策落地节奏不及预期风险，海外经济超预期波动风险。已公布的
宏观或产业政策在实际执行过程中，可能因配套措施不完善、部门协调不畅、
地方执行力度差异或市场环境变化等因素，导致其推进速度、覆盖范围或最终
效果慢于或弱于市场预期，从而影响政策目标的实现与经济修复的进程； 主要
经济体的政治局势、经济数据或宏观政策出现意外变化，其幅度或频率超出市
场普遍预测。这种超预期波动可能引发全球金融市场动荡、 贸易条件恶化或外

需急剧收缩，并通过金融、贸易及预期渠道向国内传导，增加我国外部环境的
复杂性与不稳定性。

分析师承诺  
本报告署名分析师具有中国证券业协会授予的证券投资咨询执业资格并注册为证券分析师，报告所采用的数据均
来自合法合规渠道，分析逻辑基于分析师的职业理解，通过合理判断得出结论，独立、客观地出具本报告。分析师承
诺不曾因，不因，也将不会因本报告中的具体推荐意见或观点而直接或间接获取任何形式的补偿。

投资评级说明  
报告中投资建议所涉及的评级分为公司评级和行业评级（另有说明的除外） 。评级标准为报告发布日后 6 个月内
的相对市场表现，即：以报告发布日后 6 个月内公司股价（或行业指数）相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数的涨跌幅
作为基准。其中：A 股市场以沪深 300 指数为基准， 新三板市场以三板成指 （针对协议转让标的） 或三板做市指数 （针
对做市转让标的）为基准；香港市场以恒生指数为基准；美国市场以纳斯达克综合指数或标普 500 指数为基准。  
公司评级  
买入：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅在 20%以上  
持有：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于 10%与 20%之间 
中性：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于 -10%与 10%之间  
回避：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅介于 -20%与-10%之间  
卖出：未来 6 个月内，个股相对同期相关证券市场代表性指数涨幅在 -20%以下  
行业评级  
强于大市：未来 6 个月内，行业整体回报高于同期相关证券市场代表性指数 5%以上 
跟随大市：未来 6 个月内，行业整体回报介于同期相关证券市场代表性指数 -5%与 5%之间 
弱于大市：未来 6 个月内，行业整体回报低于同期相关证券市场代表性指数 -5%以下

### Page 2

For the month of October 2026, we estimate AirTAC’s monthly revenue will decrease 
to Rmb729mn (-10% MoM, +16% YoY)/NT$3,447mn (-10% MoM, +28% YoY) 
We expect October revenue will go down by 10% MoM to Rmb729mn/NT$3,447mn, 
mainly due to 19% fewer working days in Mainland China. However, we expect to see 
improvement in daily pneumatic revenue (Rmb32.3mn in Oct vs. Rmb 29.4mn in Sept) as 
the improvement in daily pneumatic revenue is driven by the strong growth from key 
customers, including electronics, followed by machine tools and battery, and we further 
expect demand to improve following the end of low season.

Earnings and TP revisions 
We revise down our 2026E earnings estimates by 1% to factor in actual 3Q26 revenue 
(revise down 2026 revenue by 1%). We revise down our 2027E/2028E earnings estimate 
by 1%/1% to factor in a more conservative assumption on battery and linear guide 
business (revise down AirTAC revenue by 1%/1% in 2027E/2028E). We continue to see 
the company focusing on cost control as well as experiencing a better product mix 
outlook, but we expect some offset by rising material cost (revise down 2026E/2028E 
OPM by 0.1/0.1ppt).

### Page 3

Maintain our Buy rating on AirTAC, with a new 12m TP of NT$1,895 (from NT$1,925), 
based on an unchanged target P/E of 26x on 2027 EPS.

Investment view: Accelerating monthly YoY from smaller segments could be a surprise. 
Maintain positive view on AirTAC 
We reiterate our Buy rating on AirTAC as we continue to believe the company is a key 
beneficiary of the factory automation/industrial automation localization trend in 
mainland China, and we expect the company will also benefit from the prolonged 
upcycle supported by the ongoing government stimulus policies. We see multiple key 
end-segments’ performance tracking ahead of the company’s 2026 full year guidance of 
20%+ YoY growth (see here), with battery/textile/machine tools/electronics delivering 
55%/46%/41%/23% YTD YoY growth while total revenue in Rmb is tracking at 23% YTD 
YoY growth. 
We continue to see solid monthly YoY growth from smaller segments, such as energy 
lighting and textile. However, the battery segment, which is the key growth driver in 
2025 (with 89% YoY in 2025), is seeing its first negative monthly YoY growth since Oct 
2024, while other major contribution sectors, such as electronics and automotive are 
seeing decelerating monthly YoY growth. As a result, we remain cautious on the 
potential of exaggerated orders (including advance or duplicate orders) despite new 
orders intake continuing to outpace shipments, and we will continue to monitor the 
underlying end-demand closely.

### Page 4

Maintain Buy on AirTAC with a lower 12m TP of NT$1,895 (from NT$1,925). 
Investment Thesis - AirTAC 
AirTAC is the 2nd-largest pneumatic supplier in mainland China with 28% market share 
in 2025, outpacing most local competitors, owing to its low-cost yet high quality 
product, and service quality reputation in most key cities in mainland China. We forecast 
its market share will reach 30% in 2026E and expect AirTAC to be the structural winner 
in a deflationary market, due to its: (1) strong market growth and leadership: AirTAC 
continues to expand its market share in mainland China driven by competitive pricing 
that is more than 10% lower vs. international peers (with 45%+ GM / 30-35% OPM) and 
high demand elasticity among mainland China customers; and (2) superior cost structure 
and financial health: with best-in-class cash cost and a solid balance sheet, featuring 
positive net cash over FCF since 2023/2024, we believe AirTAC is well-equipped to 
weather industry downturns and capitalize on market restructuring. 
Overall, we believe AirTAC will see better revenue/profitability in the long term given its 
share gainer position in the China pneumatic market and potential to gain share in the 
linear guide market (improved demand in the next 1-2 years). This should lead to 
strengthened valuation, vs. its recent trading range (trading below its 10-year avg. P/E 
multiple). We are Buy rated on AirTAC. 
Price target risks and methodology - AirTAC 
Valuation methodology: Our 12m TP of NT$1,895 is based on a target P/E of 26.0x on 
our 2027E EPS (0.25x STDV above avg of the last pneumatic upcycle). 
Key downside risks: (1) Stronger competition from peers; (2) sharper-than-expected 
Rmb depreciation, and (3) a longer-than-expected end-market demand downtrend in 
mainland China.

Source: Company data, Goldman Sachs Research estimates, FactSet. Price as of 12 Oct 2026 close.

Ratings, coverage universe and related definitions
Buy (B), Neutral (N), Sell (S) Analysts recommend stocks as Buys or Sells for inclusion on various regional Investment Lists. Being assigned a Buy or Sell
on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.
Coverage Universe: A list of all stocks in each coverage universe is available by primary analyst, stock and coverage universe at

https://www.gs.com/research/hedge.html.

## Document 123: 261016_daiwa_ Ardentec

Source PDF: all report pdf/261016_daiwa_ Ardentec.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: We preview Ardentec’s 3Q26 results. In our initiation report, 
“Initiation: an emerging force in AI/HPC testing” (Sep 9), we expected 
traditional seasonality in 4Q26-1Q27 to weigh on Ardentec’s capacity 
utilisation. However, we now expect stronger-than-expected momentum in 
the networking segment to drive counter-seasonal resilience through 
1Q27E. We also expect AI revenue (with AI connectivity) contribution to 
exceed 40% in 1Q27E, well ahead of our original expectations.

What’s the impact: Robust networking demand bolsters high 
utilisation and CPO traction. Our industry checks indicate that demand 
for networking products is surging across the testing supply chain, primarily 
fuelled by the acceleration of 1.6T networking adoption. We forecast 
Ardentec’s revenue in 4Q26 and 1Q27 to beat typical seasonal slowdowns. 
As these high-speed networking programs largely follow a customer-
consignment model, their rising revenue mix should provide upside to 
overall gross margin. Concurrently, subsidiary Giga Solution is gaining 
momentum in co-packaged optics (CPO) testing, having already qualified 
and begun ramping up with leading US tier-1 players. While current 
engagement is centred on insertion 1, we see potential for customer base 
diversification and expansion into subsequent test insertions (2 and 3).

3Q26 results preview. With the Longtan fab expansion on track and 
spillover demand continuing, Ardentec 3Q26 revenue reached TWD5.34bn 
(+17.7% QoQ, +48.5% YoY), above the market consensus by 0.8% but 
slightly missing our estimate by 1.4%. With utilisation remaining high in 
3Q26, we estimate its gross margin and operating margin will reach 45.1% 
and 35.5%, respectively, above the consensus by 1.3pp and 1.9pp, 
respectively. We expect cleanroom construction of 2nd and 3rd floors will 
be finalised by year-end 2026 driven by solid domestic AI/HPC orders, with 
2nd-floor capacity likely contributing by late-1Q27.

What we recommend: We raise our 2027-28E EPS by 7% to reflect 
better-than-expected networking demand but lower our 2026E EPS by 1%. 
We reaffirm our Buy (1) rating and raise our 12M TP to TWD395 (from 
TWD340), based on an unchanged target PER of 25x on our 2027E EPS. 
While our target PER represents a premium over the OSAT/testing peer 
average, we believe this premium is justified by steady demand for high-
speed AI connectivity and the Longtan ramp-up. Our estimates indicate 
these factors could lift AI-related revenue to more than 40% of total sales 
by 1Q27. Key downside risk: delays in capacity expansion.

How we differ: Our 2027-28E EPS are 7-26% above the Bloomberg 
consensus, likely on our more aggressive utilisation rate and margin profile.

16 October 2026

3Q26 preview: AI defies seasonality

➢ Solid AI networking demand drives counter-seasonal resilience 
➢ On-track Longtan ramp and CPO upside expand advanced testing 
➢ Reaffirming our Buy (1) rating; raising our 12M TP to TWD395

Company profile 
Founded in 1999, Ardentec is a leading Taiwanese pure-play wafer-testing provider serving foundries, 
IDMs and fabless chipmakers. Its acquisitions of Giga Solution and Raytek over 2016-17 added RF 
testing and bumping capabilities. Combining automotive-grade reliability with flexible AI/HPC capacity, 
the company provides customers a secondary supplier which enhances supply-chain resilience.

ESG analysis

Update Date: 9 Sep 2026 
Source: Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 2
The board demonstrates strong governance oversight, with 42% independent directors, regular
performance evaluations, and ESG-linked executive compensation. Governance quality is
further supported by a top 5% ranking in Taiwan’s corporate governance assessment for nine
consecutive years.
Capital management 2
Capital allocation appears disciplined, balancing expansion investments with shareholder
returns. The company funded major capacity growth while maintaining strong profitability and
distributed stable cash dividends, reflecting a prudent and shareholder-friendly capital
management approach.
Related party &
transaction 1
Related-party transactions appear well governed and transparent. The company states that
intercompany dealings follow arm’s-length principles and are not used for tax minimization, while
directors are required to disclose conflicts and abstain from related decisions.
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

## Document 203: [207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 불확실성보다 성장을 볼 시점삼성바이오로직스(207940) 불확실성보다 성장을 볼 시점불확실성보다 성장_3801b631

Source PDF: all report pdf/[207940] 삼성바이오로직스(207940) 불확실성보다 성장을 볼 시점삼성바이오로직스(207940) 불확실성보다 성장을 볼 시점불확실성보다 성장_3801b631.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-22
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

매수 (유지)  
3분기 실적 컨센서스 상회 
삼성바이오로직스의 2026년 3분기 매출액은 1조 4,903억 원(+18.5% Y oY), 
영업이익은 6,401억 원(+1.0% Y oY, 영업이익률 43.0%)으로 시장 컨센서스를 각각 
10%, 5% 상회했다. 1~4공장의 풀가동이 이어진 가운데 5공장의 인증용 생산배치 
물량과 미국 락빌 공장 매출(약 1,000억 원)이 처음으로 반영되며 외형 성장을 
견인했다. 매출원가율은 50.4%로 전년 동기(41.4%), 전분기(47.0%) 대비 
상승했는데, 락빌 공장과 5공장의 초기 물량 반영에 따른 것으로 판단한다. 향후 
물량 상승에 따라 매출원가율은 감소할 것으로 예상한다. 그럼에도 EBITDA 
마진율은 50.4%로 높은 수준을 유지하며 7,515억 원을 달성했다. 순이익은 원/달러 
환율 하락에 따른 외화환산손실이 반영되며 3,911억 원을 기록했다.  
환율 하락에도 가이던스 상향하며 성장 궤도 유지 
2026년 연간 매출액은 5조 6,133억 원(+23.2% Y oY), 영업이익은 2조 4,605억 
원(+18.9% YoY)으로 전망한다. 최근 원/달러 환율 하락에도 이번 실적발표에서 
상향 제시한 연간 매출 성장률 20% 이상은 충분히 달성 가능할 것으로 판단한다. 
4분기에는 락빌 공장의 매출 약 1,000억 원과 5공장 가동 확대에 더해, 5월 
파업으로 이연된 물량의 매출 인식이 성장에 기여할 전망이다.  
투자의견 매수 및 목표주가 2,000,000원 유지 
비만 치료제 CDMO 진출을 위한 폴리펩타이드 그룹 인수는 공개매수 응모율 
87.88%로 최소 조건을 충족해 11월 말 완료를 앞두고 있어 신규 성장 동력은 
확보된 것으로 판단한다. 노사 리스크도 법원의 쟁의행위 제한 확대에 이어 노조가 
2차 총파업을 잠정 연기하며 크게 완화됐다. 미국 관세 불확실성으로 부진했던 신규 
수주 모멘텀은 올해 연말부터 내년에 걸쳐 강화될 것으로 전망하며, 불확실성 
해소와 성장 기대감을 반영할 시점이라 판단한다.

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• Recent revisions to US PCE inflation, UK productivity
and Canadian population estimates are reminders that
statistical measurement is imperfect. Revisions are both a
fact of life and, when they improve measurement, desirable.
• Australian data will also be revised over time, though

not necessarily in the same ways as these latest revisions
abroad. The ABS tends to favour quality over speed,
which reduces the need for early revisions as more data
become available. But revisions can still matter. Population
estimates implied in the initial releases of labour market and
GDP data are especially likely to be revised in the near term.
• Estimates of current trends can change as future data are
published. Shifting seasonal patterns are another source

of revision, especially for monthly CPI components with
short histories. Series that are derived from multiple other
imperfectly measured series are more prone to revision
than quantities that can be measured directly. All these
considerations point to a need for humility when using
recent data to make strong policy pronouncements.
On September 30, the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation,
PCE, was revised down. New methods and data sources were
applied to three components of services inflation (financial

advice, software and legal services), large enough to result in
downward revisions to the overall read on core PCE inflation.
These changes had been pre-announced in June and were
therefore not a surprise. The CPI measure of inflation was not
affected, nor should the revisions materially change your view
of US inflationary pressures.
Some revisions are more consequential for interpretation and
policy. A couple of weeks before the US PCE revision, it was

revealed that the UK Office for National Statistics was revising
down its estimates of average hours worked per worker. The
result was that measured productivity growth was nowhere
near as weak as had been widely believed. As my friend and
counterpart, TS Lombard’s Chief Economist Freya Beamish
commented on social media, “Oh well. Not like any major policy
decisions were taken on the basis of those numbers.” Quite.
Also in September, Statistics Canada revised up its estimates

of Canada’s population, taking the recent growth rates
from outright contraction to still-slow growth a little below
½%yr. The number of temporary residents in Canada is still
falling, but the revised data do a better job of tracking them,
especially those seeking an extension on their permit. (An alert
When reality gets revised
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
for customers who have seen the cross-country population
graph I often use at client events: the Canadian line now looks

different but still shows a sharp slowing.)
There is no fault to be attributed here. Revisions to key
statistics are a part of economic life. Sometimes the underlying
data arrive with a lag, perhaps because of reporting deadlines.
Sometimes the compiling agency improves its methods or
gains access to a better data source. The US and UK examples
above fall into this category. In the Canadian case, better
data and a more accurate definition of scope were involved.

All these revisions should be welcomed. They provide a more
accurate picture of the underlying reality.
Australia, revised
Revisions are a fact of life for Australian statistics, too. I am
still a little scarred by that unexpected –0.5%qtr GDP print
when the September quarter 2016 data were first released on
my first day as RBA Assistant Governor; the figure has since
been revised to 0%qtr and was followed by a big bounce-back
in the December quarter.

The recent revisions mentioned above are less likely to be
replicated in Australia, though. Australia’s CPI is designed such
that the headline (not seasonally adjusted) measure is never
revised. This is because it is used in many legal contracts.
If an error is detected, the ABS makes the adjustment in
subsequent months and quarters rather than revising the
history. Our labour force survey has a much higher response
rate than its UK equivalent – Australians are a compliant lot

when the ABS comes knocking – and measures actual hours
worked more reliably. And our population statistics already
handle the array of temporary resident visas with the Bureau
instead applying a standard 12-month-in-16-month residency
test to count as a resident for the population statistics rather
than using a visa-classification basis.
This last case illustrates Australia’s broader tendency to
favour quality over speed in official statistics. Our CPI and

national accounts data tend to be published a bit later than
peer countries’ data for the same reference period, and our
“Economic data are often revised. Sometimes
the revisions change the story; sometimes
they do not."

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
estimated resident population data is quite a bit lagged
relative to Canada’s (the 12-month-in-16 criteria taking over
a year to confirm). The trade-off is that observers of the
Australian economy must wait longer but can be less worried
about “flash” and “advance” releases of data that can be

quickly revised to tell a completely different story.
Revisions to data still can matter in Australia, and it helps
to be aware of the specific cases where it might occur. For
example, precisely because the population data are very
lagged, the population estimates underlying the initial
releases of the labour force survey and national accounts are
based on preliminary data and get revised later as a matter
of routine. This is why ratios such as the unemployment,

employment-to-population and participation rates provide a
better real-time gauge of labour market conditions than the
monthly change in employment. Similarly, GDP per capita can
be revised even when GDP is not. This type of revision is also
a more likely source of short-term changes in measured hours
worked in the Australian productivity data than the conceptual
change that shifted the UK data.
The usual reporting lags – and occasional correction of

reporting errors by the reporting entity – will always be a factor
in Australia just as it is in other countries. The annual national
accounts, due on 23 October, incorporate additional data, revise
the quarterly estimates and provide first estimates of important
quantities such as the capital stock. Just as importantly, though,
the annual accounts reconcile the different ways of measuring
GDP. In doing so, what we thought we knew about the recent

history of output growth can change.
Every purchase is also a sale, so conceptually, you should get the
same answer for final output regardless of whether you count
how much is produced, how much people spent on it, or how
much income people got for producing it. In practice, though,
the “production”, “expenditure” and “income” approaches do
not always line up until the ABS goes through a more thorough
reconciliation process in compiling the annual accounts.

When the future changes the past
Future data can also change our view of the past. Two good
examples are trend estimation and seasonal adjustment. Future
observations help reveal whether an apparent trend is stable.
As a result, real-time trend estimates can differ considerably
from those calculated with the benefit of later data.
Identifying stable seasonal patterns also requires a reasonable
run of data. Future observations affect whether an earlier

movement is judged to be normal seasonal variation. This
problem is more pronounced for newer series, where there is
not yet enough history to establish what “normal” looks like.
Consider, then, the monthly CPI. This relatively new measure has
only a short run of data with many individual components having
too little history to use standard seasonal adjustment methods.
As Westpac’s senior economist Justin Smirk has previously
highlighted, some components such as footwear are being

‘seasonally adjusted’ by applying Henderson trends, similar
to those used in the ‘trend’ measures of labour market and
national accounts data. For some other components, including
books, furniture, audio-visual and computing equipment and
children’s clothes, certain price falls – usually associated with
sales periods – are just arbitrarily removed from the seasonally
adjusted series. The index is held at its previous month’s level
but otherwise matches the non-seasonally adjusted series.

The seasonally adjusted measures of these CPI components will
inevitably be revised over time, and thus so could the monthly
trimmed mean estimates. This helps explain why the RBA has
said it would, for now, focus on the quarterly trimmed mean
based on the old seasonal factors. That measure will at least
not be buffeted by shifting measurement of seasonality in a
short history. If seasonal patterns are themselves also shifting,
the current underlying trend might look a bit different in a few

years, not just a smoother month-to-month profile.
The broader point here is to remember that economic statistics
are a lens on reality. Sometimes the lens is smudged, sometimes
the scope is off and sometimes what is being read as underlying
trends is wrong. Measures constructed from several imperfect
series – productivity from output and hours worked, or unit labour
costs from productivity and labour costs, or savings rates derived
from income and spending – are especially prone to revision

compared with concepts that can be measured more directly, like
a price. That is a strong argument for humility before making firm
policy pronouncements based on simple data comparisons.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: mulling the minutes
consistent with a labour market that is gradually normalising
than one entering a meaningful downturn.
The September FOMC minutes reinforced a distinctly hawkish
message, with participants noting they had "not seen

sufficient progress on lowering inflation in recent months" and
expressing concern that inflation risks remained "skewed to the
upside". Policymakers highlighted that both core services and
core goods inflation remained elevated, with some attributing
persistent goods price strength to the effects of the AI buildout
despite the sector's traditional disinflationary role. There was
also concern that, after more than five years of inflation above
target, elevated inflation could begin to influence inflation

expectations and wage- and price-setting behaviour. In
contrast, labour market conditions were generally viewed as
stable and "close to maximum employment". While a majority
noted the labour market had strengthened somewhat recently,
several participants highlighted unusually low dynamism,
characterised by low hiring, low layoffs, a low job-finding
rate and elevated long-term unemployment. Importantly,
participants generally judged that aggregate wage growth

was moderate and "consistent with inflation moving toward 2
percent", with several explicitly noting that "the labor market
was not currently a source of inflationary pressures".
While participants broadly expected AI-related investment to
boost productivity growth and potential output over time, several
warned that "the AI buildout could cause aggregate demand
to outpace aggregate supply over the medium term, putting
upward pressure on inflation". Consistent with this view, some

participants had raised their estimate of the neutral policy rate
and several judged the current policy stance to be "not restrictive
or only mildly restrictive". Against this backdrop, all participants
supported the September rate increase, and most assessed
that another increase in the federal funds rate would likely be
appropriate by year-end, although future decisions would remain
data dependent.
The September ISM Services PMI released this week painted

a similar picture of an economy that remains resilient but is
gradually losing momentum. The headline index eased to 54.9
from 55.4 in August, with the decline driven by a sharp fall in
business activity and a 9.4pt drop in new export orders, which
slipped into contractionary territory for the first time in eight
The only data release of note in Australia this week was
the October Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment Survey ,
which reported a 4.7% drop in the headline index. At

80.4, sentiment has tumbled back to deeply pessimistic
levels, similar to the post-pandemic cost-of-living shock
when readings were among the weakest in the survey’s
more than fifty-year history. Indeed, inflation and interest
rate pressures are once again clearly front of mind for
consumers, with both the view on ‘family finances vs a
year ago’ and ‘family finance next 12 months’ falling 8.0%
and 6.4% in the month, respectively. Moreover, the RBA’s

September rate hike looks to have had a significant impact –
sentiment tanked 20% between those surveyed before the
RBA decision and those surveyed afterwards.
Views on the economic outlook were relatively more resilient,
but they still weakened, with the one-year and five-year ahead
sub-indexes falling 1.6% and 0.4% respectively. Consumers
are becoming more ‘on edge’ about job security, albeit not
outright alarmed. The unemployment rate remains relatively

low versus history, although it has drifted up to a cycle high
of 4.6% as labour supply continues to outstrip demand. We
expect it to hold there in next week’s September Labour Force
Survey – see our preview for more detail. All told, with higher
interest rates, renewed inflation pressures and a softening
jobs market all weighing at once, households are entering the
final months of the year on the back foot.
Offshore, the focus was on US data.
Before turning to this week's releases, it is worth noting that

September non-farm payrolls came in softer than expected
across most metrics. Employment rose by only 29k (89k
expected), and there were sizeable downward revisions
to prior months, with a combined 60k removed from July
and August payrolls, leaving gains of just 31k and 29k
respectively. The details pointed to weaker hiring in leisure
& hospitality and education & health services industries,
although construction and manufacturing employment
continued to benefit from ongoing data-centre-related

investment. The unemployment rate edged up to 4.2%, with
softer employment occurring alongside a rise in labour force
participation to 61.8%. Meanwhile wage growth moderated
to 0.1%mth (3.0%yr). Taken together, the report suggests
labour demand is cooling, with the three-month average
pace of job creation slowing to around 50k. However, softer
payroll gains are not necessarily indicative of labour market
deterioration. Lower immigration and ageing demographics

have reduced the pace of employment growth needed to keep
the unemployment rate stable. As such, the report is more
Sian Fenner, Head of Business and Industry Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist
“A key focus was the interaction between
AI investment, productive capacity and
inflation."

months. Labour market conditions improved modestly with
the employment index moving to broadly neutral territory
50.1 after two months below 50, supported by elevated
backlogs and still-solid demand. Respondent commentary
illustrated the mixed nature of labour demand, with some

Overall, the report suggests services activity remains

Overall, the data supports our view that the FOMC will move

one more rate hike, although the case for a back-to-back move

### Page 6

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
6
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Fuel, housing and the bumpy road ahead
Higher fuel costs, a weaker exchange rate, and a softer
housing market have prompted a refresh of our near-term
forecasts. Inflation looks set to finish 2026 above 4%, while
growth could lose a little momentum into year-end. Even so,

we continue to expect the RBNZ to pause in October before
lifting the OCR to 3% in December.
New Zealand’s recovery is still moving forward, but the road
has become rougher. Since our last forecast update in August,
global oil and refined-fuel prices have climbed sharply, the
New Zealand dollar has fallen, and longer-term interest rates
have moved higher. At home, the housing market has remained
weaker than we anticipated. None of those developments,

on their own, are enough to derail the recovery. Together,
however, they point to a tougher finish to 2026 for households
and businesses. We have therefore revisited our near-term
inflation, housing and growth forecasts. The broad story
remains one of continued economic expansion and gradually
improving labour demand, but with a more pronounced
inflation spike and slightly less momentum in consumer
spending. Our OCR call is unchanged: a pause at the October

review, followed by a 25 basis point increase to 3% at the
December Monetary Policy Statement.
A temporary inflation reprieve
We continue to think that consumer prices rose 0.7% in the
September quarter. That would lower annual inflation from
4.1% in June to 3.7% in September, broadly consistent with
the monthly price updates published so far. The final monthly
release for the quarter is due on 16 October and will help firm up
that estimate. But any easing in the annual rate is likely to prove

short-lived. We now expect prices to rise 1.1% in the December
quarter, lifting annual inflation to a three-year high of 4.2% by
year-end. That is half a percentage point above our previous
projection and above the RBNZ’s September forecast of 3.9%.
The reason is easy to see at the petrol pump. Higher global
fuel prices have been amplified by a more than 4% fall in
the New Zealand dollar since the start of September. Local
petrol prices have risen by around 50 cents a litre over the

past month, with the nationwide average price of 91 unleaded
reaching a record $3.55 a litre this week. RBNZ Governor
Breman acknowledged a couple of weeks ago that higher
oil prices have tilted the risks around the near-term inflation
outlook to the upside. Importantly, however, the RBNZ remains
focused on the medium-term inflation outlook rather than
trying to offset every movement in volatile fuel prices.
For now, we have left our quarterly inflation forecasts for 2027

unchanged. Even so, the higher starting point at the end of this
year means annual inflation is likely to remain firm through much
of 2027. Our updated profile has inflation at 3.7% in March
2027, 2.6% in June, 2.5% in September and 1.7% in December.
That is broadly similar to the RBNZ’s path through the first three
quarters, although our forecast falls more quickly late in the
year. We will take a fuller look at the 2027 inflation and growth
outlook next week alongside the monthly Market Outlook.

Housing fails to find its feet
The housing market has also delivered a disappointment. We
had expected prices to stabilise through the second half of
2026, but sales volumes have continued to weaken, and the
median time required to sell a property has lengthened to 48
days. Average prices have kept edging lower, despite firmer
conditions in parts of the South Island with greater exposure
to the strong export economy. The latest indicators do not
suggest that a decisive turn is close. We have therefore revised

our forecast for national house price growth in 2026 from a
0.6% rise to a 1.4% decline. That incorporates quarterly falls
of 0.5% in September and 0.8% in December.
The headwinds are familiar: the labour market remains soft,
mortgage rates are higher and employment growth is still
moderate. Those forces are likely to keep the market subdued
for some time, with risks tilted towards another weak year
in 2027. Changes to the taxation of capital gains on investor

housing following the 2026 General Election may add another
potential drag.
Kelly Eckhold
Chief Economist NZ

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
7
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Regional outcomes will continue to differ. Tourism- and
agriculture-focused areas should benefit from stronger income
and employment growth, supported by robust commodity export
prices. We have lifted our 2026/27 Fonterra Milk Price forecast to
$9.75/kg and set an opening forecast of $9.50/kg for 2027/28. In

contrast, homeowners in the major North Island cities may have to
wait longer for the wealth boost that comes with rising property
values. Data released this week shows that household wealth
continues to be dragged down by declining housing values.
A little less momentum
The weaker housing backdrop matters for the wider economy.
When petrol absorbs more of the household budget and home
values are drifting lower, consumers tend to become more
cautious. That combination is likely to crimp discretionary

spending in the run-up to Christmas and could take some
steam out of the construction recovery. That’s even though our
own card spending data for September  showed resilience in
consumer spending. Recent business and consumer confidence
surveys have nevertheless remained resilient despite the rise
in energy prices and long-term interest rates. Our short-term
growth forecasts therefore remain broadly intact, but we have
trimmed our December quarter consumer spending projection

and now expect GDP growth of 2.5% over 2026. That is still
a solid result, although the balance of risks has shifted to
the downside.
There are several moving parts to watch. Sustained high fuel
prices would squeeze household purchasing power more than
we have allowed for. The sharp lift in long-term interest rates
may also weigh on activity through 2027, even if shorter-
term borrowing costs evolve as expected. The election result
introduces two-sided risks that are difficult to quantify before

the shape and timing of policy changes are known. Against that,
strong agricultural incomes and signs of improving employment
growth should provide a buffer. We have not changed our 2026
labour market forecasts. Hiring appears to be strengthening
gradually, but it will take time before that improvement
translates into a noticeable fall in the unemployment rate.
The RBNZ should still pause before Christmas
Despite the higher near-term inflation track, we are not

changing our OCR forecast. Since the July Monetary Policy
Review, our view has been that the RBNZ would slow the pace
of increases as the OCR approached the Bank’s estimate of a
neutral 3%. The September meeting clearly signalled a pause
in October and one further rise in December. We continue to
regard that as the most likely path. The latest developments
raise headline inflation but, so far, there is little evidence that
inflation expectations are moving higher or that the fuel shock

is generating broader, persistent price pressure. At the same
time, growth risks warrant some caution.
A pause in October would give the Monetary Policy Committee
time to assess the cumulative effect of the increases already
delivered and to refine its strategy for 2027. Financial
conditions have also tightened from an unexpected source:
longer-term interest rates have risen markedly over the past
six weeks. That increase has at least offset the easing impulse

from the lower exchange rate, even though the weaker
currency is adding to imported inflation in the near term. We
still think the RBNZ is likely to have more work to do in 2027,
but there is plenty of data to come before that judgement
needs to be made.
For now, the best description of the outlook is resilient but
less comfortable. Export incomes remain strong, activity is
expanding and the labour market is beginning to improve. But
households face an awkward combination of record petrol

prices, soft home values and higher borrowing costs. That
mix points to a bumpy end to the year: inflation will be hotter,
spending growth a little cooler and the recovery more uneven
across regions. The RBNZ can afford to wait in October, but if
the economy develops broadly as we expect, a further hike to
3% in December will still be needed.

+20k, which would see annual growth hold in recent ranges.
AUS: Sep Labour Force – Unemployment Rate (%)
Oct 15, Last: 4.6, Westpac f/c: 4.6 
Market f/c: 4.6, Range: 4.5 to 4.7
The participation rate jumped to 67.1% in August, just under 
the 115-year high recorded in January 2025. With labour 
supply once again outstripping demand, the unemployment 
rate rose from 4.5% to 4.6% – the highest since in late-2021, 
close to rounding up to 4.7% (4.646% at three decimal places).
A softer economy and sub-par employment growth typically 
discourages some people to search for work, but cost-of-living 
pressures and interest rate rises are more than outweighing this 
effect, encouraging more people into the labour market.
With the participation rate expected to pare back to 67.0%, we 
think the unemployment rate will hold steady at 4.6%. Please 
see our preview for more detail.

### Page 9

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.9
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
NZ: Sep REINZ House Sales and Prices (%ann)
Oct 14, Sales Last: –13.0, Prices Last: –0.9
The housing market has remained soft in recent months,
with sales falling to a two-year low and prices edging lower.
Mortgage rates have risen as the RBNZ has initiated a new

tightening cycle, and a growing supply of unsold homes on the
market means that buyers are under no pressure to bid prices
higher. Conditions remain stronger in the south, with prices
up on last year in Canterbury, Otago and Southland, while
Auckland and Wellington are the weakest markets.
NZ: Sep Retail Card Spending (%mth)
Oct 16, Last: –0.9, Westpac f/c: 0.8
We’re forecasting a 0.8% rise in retail spending in September,
reversing the 0.9% fall seen in the previous month. In part, the

forecast rise in spending is due to the sharp increases in petrol
prices in recent weeks. Those higher fuel costs are weighing on
spending in other categories like hospitality. Nevertheless, we
still expect a 0.5% rise in core (ex-fuel) spending. Stats NZ’s
monthly retail spending figures have been very choppy for the
past two years, and we’re likely to see a bounce following last
month’s unexpected falls in categories like furnishings.
NZ: Sep Selected Consumer Prices (%mth)

Oct 16
This will be the last monthly update ahead of the full quarterly
CPI release on 22 October. We’re currently forecasting a 0.7%
quarterly rise in the CPI (+3.7%yr) and will be watching the
detail of the Selected Prices update to pin down that forecast.
In terms of the big categories, we’re forecasting a roughly 6%
rise in petrol costs, along with a 0.7% rise in food prices. That’s
balanced against continued softness in housing rents (which
are the single largest component of the CPI). The key sources

of risk for our forecast are the volatile travel categories, most
of which recorded sizeable drops last month that we expect to
partially reverse in September.

MACRO STRATEGY 
MARKET VIEW 
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK 
MARKETS IN REVIEW 
Portfolio Considerations
We view recent volatility as a rolling
correction rather than the end of
the cycle. We favor diversified Equity

exposure, with potential opportunities
extending beyond Growth to Value,
Small- and Mid-caps, Non-U.S. Equities
(especially Emerging Markets), dividend-
focused and thematic strategies.
We favor adjusting portfolios toward
strategic durations given attractive
elevated nominal and real yields, and see
value in high-quality bonds, particularly
longer-dated municipal bonds.
Consider broader portfolio
diversification with potential
opportunities across industrial metals,

energy, Real/Tangible Assets, Hedge
Funds, private markets and global
strategies to capture themes from AI,
reindustrialization, infrastructure growth
and market dispersion.
All data, projections and opinions are as of the date of this report and subject to change.
I
N THIS  ISSUE
Macro Strategy—The  Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran: Bessent’s Three-Front War and
the Stakes for
Investors: Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury secretary, but
these days he looks more like America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches

from T
okyo, where he is trying to engineer a stronger Japan yen, to the U.S. Treasury
market, where he is battling to keep the 10-year yield from breaking  decisively above
5%, and to T
ehran, where he has become the point man in the escalating U.S. campaign
to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Ho
w Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A decisive victory on
all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets is next to impossible,

while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best laid plans. That said, however,
the U.S. Secretary of the Treasury has signaled that he is ready to take a very  proactive,
pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less encouraging news stems
from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the Treasury secretary.
Market View—Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”: Behind
ever
y Artificial Intelligence (AI)-generated response is a vast physical network of data

centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems, semiconductor facilities
and critical-mineral supply chains. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power,
water, advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and
intensify pressure
on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend
beyon
d AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
Thought of the Week—The Beauty of September: Call it an eventful first 13 trading

days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per barrel, diesel prices surpassed
$6
per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield breached 5%, and
the Federal Reserve (Fed) joined the European Central Bank (ECB) and the Bank of Japan
(BoJ) in hiking rates for the
first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has
shed 1.4% since the start of the month. It’s not unexpected that September would be
choppy for U.S. Equities, especially in a midterm election year. The good news is that

this has historically been an attractive entry point. Since 1950, the S&P 500 has never
been
negative in the six or twelve months post-midterm election, boasting average
returns of 13.8% and 15.8%, respectively. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election. A correction amid a strong fundamental economic backdrop
would be a
potential buying opportunity in our view.
Chartered Financial Analyst® and CFA® are
registered trademarks owned by CFA Institute.

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran:  Bessent’s Three-Front War and the Stakes for Investors
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury Secretary, but these days he looks more like
America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches from Tokyo, where he is trying
to engineer a stronger Japan yen; to the U.S. Treasury market, where he is battling to keep

the 10-year yield from breaking decisively above 5%; and Tehran, where he has become the
point man in the escalating U.S. campaign to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Make no mistake about it: Secretary Bessent and the Treasury Department are increasingly
an instrument of American economic statecraft, charged not simply with financing the
government and overseeing the financial system, but with managing markets, currencies
and geopolitical adversaries simultaneously. Welcome to the new age of financial warfare.

Front One: Defending the Yen. The first front unfolded over the summer when Secretary
Bessent and the BoJ stealthily intervened in support of the yen, which had fallen to a near
40-year low against the dollar by late July. To arrest the yen’s decline, the U.S. Treasury sold
euros instead of dollars in support of the yen, while pushing the BoJ to make use of the
Fed’s emergency repo facilities. In further support of the yen, the BoJ did what Bessent and

the markets have been pining for, raising its official rate by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%.
Since intervening in the foreign exchange markets, the yen has appreciated by nearly 5%
against the U.S. dollar, alleviating concerns of a disorderly unwind of the yen and giving
Bessent—thus far—an important victory. Defending the yen, of course, isn’t simply about
Japan—it’s about the U.S. and the need for foreign capital inflows.
Japan is one of America’s largest foreign creditors. A persistently weak yen creates

pressure on Tokyo to defend its currency, potentially by selling some of its enormous
holdings of foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys. That prospect didn’t sit well with the
Treasury secretary. At precisely the moment Washington needs foreign capital to finance
its enormous fiscal deficits, the last thing Mr. Bessent wants is one of America’s largest
creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities. Hence, the battle to backstop

Japan’s currency.
Front Two: Defending the 10-Year Bond. Mr. Bessent has had less success on the
second front: the U.S. bond market. While the 10-year Treasury yield slipped slightly
following last week’s decision by the Fed to boost the fed funds rate by 25 bps, the
benchmark interest rate is up roughly 85 bps since the start of the year. Stubbornly high
inflation, elevated oil prices, enormous government borrowing requirements and growing
investor anxiety over America’s fiscal trajectory have all converged and conspired to keep

the 10-year yield closer to 5% than 4%.
Field Marshal Bessent is very aware that the 10-year Treasury is the gravitational center of
global finance—and that mortgage rates, corporate borrowing costs, equity valuations and
the government’s own interest bill all take their cue from it. A sustained move materially
above 5% would tighten financial conditions across the American economy, potentially
threaten the Equity bull market, and pile more pressure on the Republican Party.

To prevent this from happening, the Treasury has expanded its bond-buyback operations,
including purchases of longer-dated government securities, in an effort to improve liquidity
and relieve some pressure on the market, while shifting new government debt issuance
into short-term bills. Yet the bond vigilantes have so far proved harder to intimidate than
currency traders. Even after Treasury announced additional purchases, yields continued
climbing before receiving some relief from the latest hawkish move from the Fed.

Over the medium term, if investors believe Washington is borrowing too much, inflation is
running too hot or the supply of bonds is overwhelming global demand, they will demand a
higher yield to own America’s debt. This battle is far from over.
Front Three: Squeezing Tehran. Then there is Iran, where Mr. Bessent is wielding an
entirely different weapon: America’s control over the plumbing of global finance. In practice
Investment Implications
We expect continued investor

support and preference for real hard
assets, and a bias towards a plethora
of commodities and industrial plays.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
this means the Treasury can make a foreign bank choose between doing business
with Iran or the U.S. It can freeze assets, blacklist companies, restrict transactions and
pressure allies to close financial escape routes.
Under “Operation Economic Outcast,” billed as economic “D-Day,” Treasury has
intensified sanctions against Iranian entities and financial networks, including recent

actions against Iran aviation and additional efforts aimed at banks and organizations
facilitating Tehran’s international transactions. Mr. Bessent has described the objective
in unusually stark terms: to “economically asphyxiate” the Iran regime. This is economic
warfare in its purest form, but whether it works remains to be seen.
“Asphyxiating” an economy takes time and patience—and international cooperation
and coordination. It means closing multiple global loopholes of third-party entities still

willing and able to do business with Iran—look no further than Russian banks. In addition,
squeezing a major oil producer runs the risk of aggravating global oil supplies, keeping
oil prices elevated. Higher energy costs, in turn, feed right back into the U.S. inflation
outlook the Fed is trying to tame and keeps the bond vigilantes on guard and ready to
push back. Not unlike the battle to suppress U.S. bond yields, the battle to bring Iran to
heel is going to be a long one.

How does it all end? How Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A
decisive victory on all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets
is next to impossible, while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best-laid plans.
That said, however, the U.S. Treasury Secretary has signaled that he is ready to take
a very proactive, pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less
encouraging news stems from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the

Treasury secretary.
Mr. Bessent can pressure Iran because access to the dollar-based financial system
remains extraordinarily valuable. He can intervene in foreign-exchange markets because
America’s financial resources give Washington enormous clout. And he can attempt to
influence the Treasury market because U.S. government securities remain the foundation
of the global financial system. But therein lies the contradiction.
The U.S. is simultaneously weaponizing the financial system abroad while becoming more

dependent on that same financial system at home. Washington wants the world to fear
losing access to dollars while also asking the world to lend it trillions of dollars. It wants to
use Treasury and dollar dominance as instruments of geopolitical coercion while running
fiscal deficits that require foreigners to continue believing deeply in those very assets.
The more Washington borrows, the more sensitive it becomes to bond-market discipline.
The more aggressively it weaponizes access to the dollar, the greater the incentive for

adversaries to seek alternatives. And the more the Treasury secretary intervenes directly
in markets, the more investors may begin wondering where economic policy ends and
market management begins.

For the moment, Mr. Bessent is fighting on three fronts. He wants a stronger yen, a
lower 10-year Treasury yield and an economically isolated Iran. Success on any one front
would be difficult. Achieving all three simultaneously will require considerable skill—and
perhaps a little luck.

MARKET VIEW
Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”
Anna Potts, CFA, Vice President and Investment Strategist
Since the launch of ChatGPT, investors have celebrated AI’s potential while growing
more concerned about its resource footprint: Behind every AI-generated response is
a physical network of data centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems,

semiconductor facilities and critical-mineral supply chains. No doubt, the AI revolution
has environmental costs. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power, water,
advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and intensify
pressure on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend beyond
AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
AI’s footprint is large, growing and certainly not trivial. AI increases electricity demand,

adds large, concentrated loads to already constrained grids, and may slow the retirement
of polluting energy assets. U.S. data centers could account for 20% of national electricity
consumption by 2035, up from 5.9% today, with power needs reaching 194 gigawatts
(comparable to the electricity demand of more than 150 million homes). 1 Cooling, power
generation and semiconductor manufacturing require water; each new megawatt also
embeds substantial copper, aluminum and other materials; and short hardware cycles

can increase electronic waste. Use varies sharply by workload: Complex reasoning and
agentic tasks can consume many times more energy than a simple text prompt. The
effects are already reaching consumers and communities. Wholesale power costs on
PJM—the largest U.S. grid—rose 76% year-over-year in Q1 2026, while data-center
load included in its previous two capacity auctions added an estimated $13.8 billion to
customer bills. 2 Communities are mobilizing around their concerns, including affordability,

water use and air pollution: Q1 2026 produced the largest single quarterly concentration
of delayed or blocked data-center projects on record. 3 Opposition is therefore becoming
a development constraint to be considered.
Context, caveats and comparisons. Headlines often tell only part of the story. Globally,
data centers account for about 1% of electricity use, 4 modest relative to many industries,
and 9% of estimated global 2025 to 2030 electricity growth. 5 A typical text prompt on

one major company’s AI model 6 uses 0.24 watt-hours of electricity, emits 0.03 grams
of CO₂ and consumes 0.26 milliliters of water—roughly 617,000 prompts match the
emissions from producing and cooking one sirloin steak, while 262,000 match the water
used in an eight-minute shower. 7 Still, billions of interactions, more complex workloads
and growth in resource-constrained regions can create meaningful cumulative impacts.
The most meaningful assessment therefore depends on workload, utilization, location,

grid mix, cooling design, local water availability and who bears the cost of supporting
infrastructure, not one universal number.
AI’s Handprint. While much remains to be seen, AI’s environmental “handprint”—the
benefits it can enable—could potentially prove larger than its direct footprint in some
areas. Across the energy system, AI can help utilities better match electricity supply
and demand, balance grids, optimize storage and reduce wasted energy. Data centers

already account for more than 30% of corporate renewable power-purchase agreements,
helping create long-dated demand for clean generation. 8 Beyond power, AI can improve
precision agriculture, streamline transportation and inventories, and support product

investment in power generation,

grid modernization, energy storage,
advanced cooling, water treatment
and reuse, efficient computing, and
materials recovery and recycling.
Diversified environmental and thematic
strategies may offer exposure to
these physical enablers while reducing
reliance on benchmark names.

design, predictive maintenance, waste sorting and recycling. AI is transforming climate
risk management by using satellite imagery, weather data and predictive analytics to
forecast floods, wildfires and extreme weather events, enabling governments, companies,
and investors to anticipate risks and allocate capital more effectively (Exhibit 2). These
gains are not automatic: they depend on innovation, adoption, and whether efficiency

improvements reduce total resource use rather than simply stimulate more demand. All
industries are faced with the opportunity to responsibly use AI to make operations more
efficient, and much is yet to be discovered.

AI’s potential handprint becomes more tangible in transportation, agriculture and climate
resilience. In aviation, for example, AI can combine weather forecasts, flight paths and
atmospheric conditions to reroute aircraft around regions where warming contrails
are most likely to form—reducing climate impact without requiring a new aircraft or

fuel system. On farms, machine learning can interpret aerial imagery and field-level
sensor data to identify crop stress early, directing water, fertilizer and chemicals to
where they are needed and helping growers improve yields while reducing inputs. AI
can also strengthen climate preparedness by analyzing satellite imagery, weather data
and infrastructure exposure to detect deforestation, anticipate floods or wildfires,
and identify communities, facilities and supply chains most vulnerable to physical

hazards. These are just a few examples that illustrate how AI’s value can extend beyond
incremental efficiency gains to reshape real-world decisions.
Investment Considerations. The challenges created by AI also create potential
investment opportunities. Companies are investing in efficient chips, closed-loop cooling,
reclaimed-water systems, energy storage, grid upgrades and cleaner firm power to
reduce resource intensity and improve reliability. Professionally managed environmental

and thematic strategies can target these “picks and shovels” of the digital buildout,
alongside businesses using AI to improve efficiency across energy management,
water infrastructure, environmental services, waste management and compute. A
disciplined thematic approach can diversify exposure beyond the largest benchmark
constituents while positioning for structural growth in power demand, grid modernization
and resource efficiency. Focusing solely on AI’s footprint risks overlooking both the

companies helping it scale more efficiently and the broader environmental benefits its
handprint may enable.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The Beauty of September
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Call it an eventful first 13 trading days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per
barrel, diesel prices surpassed $6 per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S.
Treasury yield breached 5%, 9 and the Fed joined the ECB and the BoJ in hiking rates for

the first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has shed 1.4% since the start
of the month, while its equal-weighted counterpart is down by 3.3%.
It’s not all too unexpected that September would be a choppy month for U.S. Equities.
Historical seasonal patterns suggest as much. Since 1928, the average S&P 500 return
in September has come out to -1.1%, 10 not only the worst month of the year on average
but also the only month when the benchmark has ended lower more often than it’s

ended higher. Similar weakness has been seen in midterm election years, with the S&P
500 producing an average September return of -0.8% since 1950. 10
The good news is that this has historically been an attractive entry point for U.S. Equity
investors. Call it the beauty of September: In contrast to September’s average decline,
average returns improve in October (+3.0%), November (+2.7%), and December (+0.8%).
Indeed, the S&P 500 has never been negative in the six or 12 months post-midterm

election since 1950 (Exhibit 4A), boasting an impressive average return of 13.8% and
15.8% over those respective periods. 10
Looking ahead to the coming weeks, we have little reason to believe that 2026
will diverge from the historical trend. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election, in line with what’s typical in the months surrounding the
midterms (Exhibit 4B). A quieter corporate calendar ahead of Q3 earnings means

investor focus will remain on incoming macro data, the path of energy prices, and the
Fed’s policy path.
In the meantime, while the S&P 500 still stands just 2.7% below its mid-August high,
churn beneath the surface already appears to be taking place. Just 38% of S&P 500
members now stand above their 50-day moving average versus 70% a mere month ago. 9
Far from worrisome, a correction in the near term in the context of a strong fundamental
economic backdrop would be a potential buying opportunity in our view.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.
Nonfinancial assets, such as closely held businesses, real estate, fine art, oil, gas and mineral properties, and timber, farm and ranch land, are complex in nature and involve risks including total
